2. The A.L.I.C.E. we promised is still the A.L.I.C.E. we are building
I compared the old conversations, the handoff, and today's repository rather than judging the current work in isolation.
The original capability-first correction explicitly rejected the idea of A.L.I.C.E. as a small SQLite/local chatbot. The handoff defines
the destination as a continually learning, model-independent, long-horizon cognitive system with durable identity, lifelong
memory, source-person/owner/self/world/causal/social models, autonomous research and action, self-improvement, parametric
learning, multimodal perception, distributed intelligence, persistent operation, deep project continuity, and independent
judgment. ALICE_CAPABILITY_FIRST_MEMORY_MASTER_HANDOFF_2026-08-05.md
It is explicitly owner-sovereign, local-capable, deployment-unbounded. No particular database, cloud topology, model
architecture, training method, graph engine, workflow engine, or context size is supposed to become a permanent ceiling.
ALICE_CAPABILITY_FIRST_MEMORY_MASTER_HANDOFF_2026-08-05.md

That is still exactly what the current README says. A.L.I.C.E. is described as a long-term personal cognitive system, “not one
model”, whose continuity resides above whatever local, external, specialist, coding, or future reasoning engines happen to be
used. (GitHub)


The identity promise also survived intact
The current owner-ratified architecture says unambiguously:

    Mehejabin Elaina is the source person.
    A.L.I.C.E. is an Elaina-derived clone, but is not literally Elaina.
    Rayan is owner/host.
    Friday is a separate product.
    Rayan's ordinary host data cannot silently rewrite the Elaina-derived personality anchor.
    A.L.I.C.E.'s own later life remains distinct from Elaina's historical life. (GitHub)

And the clone standard is stronger than merely giving a chatbot selected traits. The explicit engineering objective is the highest-
fidelity achievable cognitive and personality reconstruction supported by the evidence. Unknown Elaina states remain
unknown rather than being quietly replaced by generic model behavior. (GitHub)
The fidelity rule we hardened during Stage G is therefore consistent with the public architecture: when evidence supports
particular sharpness, warmth, confrontation, restraint, privacy, stubbornness, intensity, humor, affection, etc., evaluation should
preserve that behavior rather than reward generic assistant mannerisms. At the same time, invented extremity is no more faithful
than invented niceness. The objective is the evidence-supported Elaina behavior. ChatGPT-Alice Memory Continuation.pdf
That distinction matters enormously.

This was one of the things I checked most carefully.
The architecture still separates at least:

The current repo explicitly makes those distinctions. (GitHub)
Personality weights are supposed to learn how the reconstructed Elaina-derived identity interprets, judges,
communicates and responds. They are deliberately not the sole record of what historically happened to Elaina. Canonical
source evidence remains independently inspectable for correction, retraining, provenance and historical truth. (GitHub)
That means the permanent distinction we developed is still essential:

  E0       = actual/owner-attested Elaina evidence
  E-INF    = evidence-conditioned inference about Elaina
  A-SYN    = synthetic Alice identity completion
  A-EXP    = actual post-activation Alice experience
  A-SELF   = Alice's consolidated current self


Those streams may influence one another through explicit lineage. They cannot silently collapse into one provenance class.

7. Friday is still correctly downstream
The current README still enforces the flagship rule: Alice is the upstream research system. Friday receives transferable
capabilities only after Alice implements, evaluates and approves them, while Alice's memories, identity, learned private weights,
relationship state and credentials do not transfer. (GitHub)
So none of the Elaina identity-completion work should ever become Friday's default personality data.
That promise is intact.

And the previous failures teach four rules I will keep enforcing:
Do not infer JSON schemas. We had repeated failures because a builder assumed field names from another artifact instead of
reading the actual upstream bytes.
Do not treat stderr as failure. Git's Windows CRLF warnings can appear on stderr with exit code 0.
Do not casually destroy failed state. A failed run can be preflight-only, partially materialized, or checkpointed. Recovery has
to inspect it first.
Your Windows PowerShell parser is final syntax authority. My static checks are additional checks, not substitutes for
[System.Management.Automation.Language.Parser]::ParseFile(...) .

The recovered MC10B1 script follows these patterns much better than the earlier problematic scripts.

continuing Alice identity. (GitHub)

performs the evidence binding, falsification, judging and eventual training.

all; we should first build/train an Alice-specific generation component and use that instead.

Yes. Under exactly the interpretation you gave, I approve using an external pretrained model as the MC10 hypothesis
generator. And between that and Option B right now, I think the external-generator route is substantially better for the mission.
But I want to make one refinement: I approve the role, not blindly qwen3:8b as the permanent best model for that role.
The architecture should be:

 FROZEN ELAINA EVIDENCE PACKET
         │
         ▼
 External pretrained LLM(s)
         │
         │ ONLY propose possibilities
         │
         ▼
 RAW E-INF HYPOTHESES
         │
         ▼
 A.L.I.C.E.'s evidence/provenance system
         │
         ├── contradiction testing
         ├── hidden-E0 reconstruction
         ├── counterevidence
         ├── contextual consistency
         ├── relationship consistency
         ├── temporal consistency
         ├── behavioral consistency



                                          🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                    14 / 188
          ├── provenance firewall
          └── UNKNOWN competitor
          │
          ▼
  surviving E-INF
          │
          ▼
  competing A-SYN creation
          │
          ▼
  massive simulation + falsification
          │
          ▼
  accepted A-SYN
          │
          ▼
  IDENTITY COMPLETION GRAPH
          │
          ▼
  eventual Alice personality training


The external model stops at “here are plausible hypotheses worth testing.”
It gets zero votes about what is true, zero authority over Alice's personality, zero authority over memory, zero training
authority, zero continuity role, and zero place in Alice's identity.


Why this is better than Option B right now
Option B would mean building an Alice-specific hypothesis generator before we have completed the personality dataset that the
generator is supposed to help us create.
That's circular.
Right now we have high-quality E0 evidence and an increasingly sophisticated graph, but the whole reason MC10 exists is that
Elaina evidence does not directly cover every behavioral combination. Training our own generator primarily from that incomplete
material would encourage it to reproduce the same holes we're trying to fill, while a large pretrained foundation model already
possesses broad reasoning, analogy, causal reasoning, counterfactual reasoning, language understanding, and knowledge of
human behavioral patterns.
The external model can therefore use the Elaina constraints we provide to propose possibilities in spaces where our raw
corpus is sparse.
Then our system decides whether those proposals actually survive Elaina-specific evidence.
That separation is powerful.
And Qwen3-8B is legitimately suited to proposal generation: its official model materials specifically emphasize reasoning,
instruction following, creative generation, role-playing and dialogue capabilities. (GitHub)
But-and this matters-the current qwen3:8b is no longer the newest Qwen generation. Qwen now has Qwen3.5 and Qwen3.6 open
models, including newer 9B, 27B and 35B-A3B options. (GitHub)
So I would not make this architectural rule:

  Qwen3-8B generates Alice's synthetic personality forever.

I'd make this rule:

  The best empirically qualified external proposal generators generate candidate hypotheses. No external
  generator acquires Alice identity authority.

That is much stronger.

And for something this important, I think we should go further

                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                        15 / 188
You are right about the significance of the synthetic-data stage.
If this works, we're not merely filling some JSON rows. We're trying to solve something much harder:

  Given incomplete observations of one specific human being, can we reconstruct enough of the missing conditional behavioral
  manifold that a learned system responds to novel situations in ways recognizably characteristic of that person?

We cannot know in advance that synthetic completion will yield a “perfect clone”; finite evidence fundamentally limits what can
be established about unobserved Elaina states. Our own architecture correctly preserves that distinction. But we can
empirically test how much better reconstruction becomes, because MC8 deliberately created hidden real-Elaina cases
whose answers we already know. ChatGPT-Alice Memory Continuation.pdf
That gives us an unusually useful opportunity.
Instead of asking:

  Is Qwen good?


we can ask:

  Can Qwen propose the missing behavior
  when we deliberately hide real Elaina evidence?

  And when it can:
  does its proposal look like the actual hidden Elaina answer?

  And does another generator do better?


That is the experiment I want.


Therefore I would slightly strengthen MC10B1
The existing pilot uses:

  5 real completion packets
  × 4 reasoning methods
  × 3 seeds
  = 60 Qwen hypotheses


That's fine as an execution smoke test.
But before we unleash generation over all 65 packets, I want a generator qualification benchmark using MC8 pseudo-gaps.
For example:

  Known Elaina behavior
          │
          ├── hide relevant E0
          ▼
  pseudo-gap
          │
          ├──────── Qwen3-8B
          ├──────── newer Qwen model if locally feasible
          ├──────── another independent model family
          └──────── UNKNOWN
                      │
                      ▼
  generate hypotheses
                      │
                      ▼
  compare against hidden real E0


Measure things like:


                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                       16 / 188
  exact/near behavioral recovery
  directional recovery
  relationship-scope fidelity
  context fidelity
  state fidelity
  false confident invention
  UNKNOWN calibration
  E0 contradiction rate
  generic-assistant flattening
  Elaina-distinctiveness
  cross-method diversity


We let actual hidden Elaina evidence tell us which generator is best.
That is vastly more defensible than me telling you from generic LLM benchmarks that Qwen must be best.
There is also another architectural improvement I'd make before mass generation: the current 4 methods × 3 seeds provide
reasoning-method diversity, but if all 12 candidates come from one underlying model, their errors can still be highly
correlated. Our v1.1 doctrine already recognizes this problem on the judging side: generator and judge cannot self-confirm, and
higher-confidence judgments require genuine independence. ChatGPT-Memory Shadow Migration G(1).pdf
For something as important as synthetic personality reconstruction, I think the eventual generator should likewise become
something closer to:

  METHOD DIVERSITY
  +
  MODEL-FAMILY DIVERSITY
  +
  SEED DIVERSITY


rather than merely:

  same Qwen
  × 4 prompts
  × 3 seeds


That doesn't mean changing MC10B1 before its smoke test necessarily. It means Qwen3-8B should earn the right to
participate in the full generator ensemble.

So your approval is recorded in my working interpretation as
Approved:

  External pretrained models may be used solely as stateless, replaceable proposal generators for E-INF/A-SYN hypothesis
  construction when doing so improves reconstruction quality. They are not part of A.L.I.C.E., do not define her identity, do not
  own memory or personality state, do not make acceptance decisions, and receive no production authority.

And importantly:

  Use of Qwen is conditional on empirical utility. If another generator or an eventual Alice-native generator
  reconstructs held-out Elaina behavior better, Qwen loses.

That matches your condition exactly.
For the current step, I choose the external-generator route over Option B. I would not train an Alice-native generator yet.
We don't have the completed training substrate for one, and doing so now risks making the gap-filler inherit the very gaps we're
trying to eliminate.
The eventual Alice personality model is still ours. Qwen is scaffolding used while constructing its training substrate. Once the
completion-aware EIPM exists, Qwen can disappear tomorrow and not one bit of Alice's identity disappears with it.

five-packet pilot gives us enough information to qualify the generator, and if not, strengthen MC10B1 so the first real generation
run simultaneously establishes a measurable baseline for whether Qwen is actually helping rather than merely producing
plausible prose.

Its exact SHA-256 is:

  35BB273507E7352159AAE02D5B72283BB37C4DC3EFA3B141D974E1E7366378EE


Run this in Windows PowerShell from your machine. This wrapper verifies the exact bytes and lets the Windows PowerShell
parser be the final syntax authority before anything executes:

  Set-Location C:\A.L.I.C.E-main

  $Script = Join-Path $HOME "Downloads\Run-ALICEMC10B1ActualEInfGenerationPilotV1-v1.0.0.ps1"
  $ExpectedHash = "35BB273507E7352159AAE02D5B72283BB37C4DC3EFA3B141D974E1E7366378EE"

  if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Script -PathType Leaf)) {
      throw "Script not found: $Script"
  }

  $ActualHash = (Get-FileHash -LiteralPath $Script -Algorithm SHA256).Hash.ToUpperInvariant()

  if ($ActualHash -ne $ExpectedHash) {
      throw "SHA-256 mismatch. Expected=$ExpectedHash Actual=$ActualHash"
  }

  Write-Host "Script verification passed."

  $Tokens = $null
  $ParseErrors = $null

  [System.Management.Automation.Language.Parser]::ParseFile(
      $Script,
      [ref]$Tokens,
      [ref]$ParseErrors
  ) | Out-Null

  if (@($ParseErrors).Count -gt 0) {
      $ParseErrors | Format-List Message, Extent
      throw "Parser check failed. Script was NOT executed."
  }

  Write-Host "Parser check passed."

  Unblock-File -LiteralPath $Script -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue

  & powershell.exe `
      -NoProfile `
      -ExecutionPolicy Bypass `
      -File $Script `
      -RepoRoot "C:\A.L.I.C.E-main" `
      -VaultRoot "C:\ALICE_Vault" `
      -ExpectedHead "0abaed85873c3f8de04765847eb7700b0e20433f" `
      -GeneratorModel "qwen3:8b" `
      -OllamaBaseUrl "http://127.0.0.1:11434" `
      -TimeoutSeconds 900

  if ($LASTEXITCODE -ne 0) {

Good catch. This failed safely and exactly where the smoke test was supposed to protect us. No E-INF was generated, no
MC10B1 package was published, no A-SYN happened, no training happened, and the repo never got past REPO_BASELINE .
The cause is precise: my v1.0.0 smoke test contained Python code with an embedded quoted string:

  import sys; assert sys.argv[1]=="a b"


Windows PowerShell 5.1's native executable argument forwarding stripped/mangled those embedded quotes before python.exe
received the -c argument. Python therefore saw something equivalent to:

  assert sys.argv[1]==a


which produced exactly your:

  NameError: name 'a' is not defined


So this is not an Alice architecture/data failure. It's a Windows PowerShell 5.1 quoting bug in my launcher script. The script
intentionally stopped before doing anything consequential. The source confirms that this smoke test occurs before the MC10B1
source-authority and generation stages. Run-ALICEMC10B1ActualEInfGenerationPilotV1-v1.0.0.ps1
I patched only two things: the header version and that smoke test. The new test still verifies that "a b" arrives as one native
argument, but its Python code contains no embedded string quotes:

  import sys; assert len(sys.argv)==2 and list(map(ord,sys.argv[1]))==[97,32,98]


I also rechecked the whole resulting file. The embedded MC10B1 builder and independent reconciler are byte-for-byte
unchanged and both still parse/compile successfully.

Do not delete or alter any Alice artifacts. Do not patch MC10B1 again. The script is behaving correctly now.
Run this exact PowerShell block first. It will find Ollama, start the local server if it is stopped, wait for the API, verify qwen3:8b , and
pull it only if it is missing.

  $ErrorActionPreference = "Stop"

  Write-Host "`n===== OLLAMA READINESS FOR MC10B1 ====="

  $Cmd = Get-Command ollama.exe -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue

  if ($null -ne $Cmd) {
      $Ollama = $Cmd.Source
  }
  else {
      $Candidate = Join-Path $env:LOCALAPPDATA "Programs\Ollama\ollama.exe"

      if (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Candidate -PathType Leaf) {
          $Ollama = $Candidate
      }
      else {
          throw "Ollama is not installed or could not be located."
      }
  }

  Write-Host "ollama_exe=$Ollama"

  function Get-OllamaVersion {
      try {
          return Invoke-RestMethod `
              -Method Get `
              -Uri "http://127.0.0.1:11434/api/version" `
              -TimeoutSec 3
      }
      catch {
          return $null
      }
  }

  $Version = Get-OllamaVersion

  if ($null -eq $Version) {
      Write-Host "ollama_api_running=false"
      Write-Host "Starting Ollama server..."

      Start-Process `
          -FilePath $Ollama `
          -ArgumentList "serve" `
          -WindowStyle Hidden

      $Deadline = (Get-Date).AddSeconds(45)

      do {
             Start-Sleep -Seconds 1
             $Version = Get-OllamaVersion
      }
      while (($null -eq $Version) -and ((Get-Date) -lt $Deadline))
  }

  if ($null -eq $Version) {
      $Log = Join-Path $env:LOCALAPPDATA "Ollama\server.log"



                                          🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                27 / 188
      Write-Host "ollama_server_log=$Log"

      throw "Ollama API did not become available on 127.0.0.1:11434."
  }

  Write-Host "ollama_api_running=true"
  Write-Host ("ollama_version=" + $Version.version)

  $Tags = Invoke-RestMethod `
      -Method Get `
      -Uri "http://127.0.0.1:11434/api/tags" `
      -TimeoutSec 30

  $Model = @($Tags.models) |
      Where-Object {
          $_.name -eq "qwen3:8b" -or
          $_.model -eq "qwen3:8b"
      } |
      Select-Object -First 1

  if ($null -eq $Model) {
      Write-Host "qwen3_8b_installed=false"
      Write-Host "Pulling qwen3:8b..."

      & $Ollama pull "qwen3:8b"

      if ($LASTEXITCODE -ne 0) {
          throw "ollama pull qwen3:8b failed with exit code $LASTEXITCODE"
      }

      $Tags = Invoke-RestMethod `
          -Method Get `
          -Uri "http://127.0.0.1:11434/api/tags" `
          -TimeoutSec 30

      $Model = @($Tags.models) |
          Where-Object {
              $_.name -eq "qwen3:8b" -or
              $_.model -eq "qwen3:8b"
          } |
          Select-Object -First 1
  }

  if ($null -eq $Model) {
      throw "qwen3:8b is still not visible after pull."
  }

  Write-Host "qwen3_8b_installed=true"
  Write-Host ("qwen3_8b_digest=" + $Model.digest)
  Write-Host ("qwen3_8b_size_bytes=" + $Model.size)
  Write-Host "mc10b1_ollama_readiness_passed=true"

qwen3:8b ; its standard package is about 5.2 GB, so don't be surprised if the first pull takes a while. (Ollama)

Yes - you caught an important point. The current MC10B1 script is not choosing the best model. It is explicitly defaulted to
 qwen3:8b ; the model is just a parameter supplied to the pilot. Run-ALICEMC10B1ActualEInfGenerationPilotV1-v1.0.0.ps1

So don't pull qwen3:8b yet. I don't want us spending the most important synthetic-personality stage using an 8B model simply
because that happened to be the first model I put into the pilot.
What we should do is better:

 Candidate generator qualification
               │
       ┌───────┼───────────┐
       ▼       ▼            ▼
  Qwen model A Qwen model B other model family
       │       │            │
       └───────┼───────────┘
               ▼
       SAME MC8 PSEUDO-GAPS
               │
       hidden real Elaina E0
               │
               ▼
    objective fidelity scoring
               │
               ▼
  BEST MODEL / BEST ENSEMBLE
               │
               ▼
        real MC10 E-INF


In other words, the algorithm should choose, but not dynamically and blindly while generating Alice's personality. We should
first conduct a controlled qualification tournament, then freeze the winning generator configuration for that MC10 round.
And we now have significantly newer Qwen options than qwen3:8b . Ollama currently offers Qwen3.5 models at 9B, 27B, 35B and
larger sizes; qwen3.5:9b is about 6.6 GB and qwen3.5:27b about 17 GB. Qwen3.5 is positioned as a broader general-purpose
family with stronger reasoning, RL generalization and multilingual coverage. (Ollama) There is also Qwen3.6 at 27B and 35B,
although its published improvements emphasize agentic coding and preservation of reasoning context, which does not
automatically mean it is the best personality-hypothesis generator. (Ollama)
That's precisely why I don't want to pick based on version number.


What should determine the winner
For Alice, standard benchmarks like math, coding or MMLU are secondary. The qualification score should heavily weight
something much closer to:



                                      🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                           30 / 188
  Hidden-Elaina reconstruction accuracy
          +
  behavioral direction accuracy
          +
  context/state accuracy
          +
  relationship-conditioned accuracy
          +
  emotional-response fidelity
          +
  value/preference fidelity
          +
  contradiction avoidance
          +
  Elaina distinctiveness
          +
  uncertainty calibration
          +
  correct UNKNOWN selection
          -
  generic assistant behavior
          -
  sycophancy
          -
  invented biography
          -
  safe-neutral personality flattening


And because MC8 already contains cases where we deliberately hide E0 while retaining the true Elaina answer for
evaluation, we have something much better than a generic benchmark: Elaina herself becomes the benchmark.
That is the right way to choose.
Also, I don't think the end result necessarily needs to be one generator. Qwen3 already comes in 8B, 14B, 30B and 32B local
variants; Ollama lists the 30B at about 19 GB. (Ollama) The strongest architecture might ultimately be something like:

  Qwen3.5-27B
       +
  another independently trained model family
       +
  different reasoning procedures
       +
  multiple seeds
       ↓
  diverse candidate pool


That would be preferable if the held-out tests show genuinely complementary error patterns. A single 27B model generating
twelve variants can still make the same underlying mistake twelve different ways.
So I am changing my recommendation at this point:
Do not rerun MC10B1 with qwen3:8b yet.
The next thing I want us to build is MC10B0 / Generator Qualification (the exact artifact name can follow our existing naming
conventions rather than me casually inventing one). It should take a leakage-safe sample of MC8 pseudo-gaps, run candidate
generator models under identical evidence and prompts, hide the evaluator E0 completely, and then independently score each
against the withheld Elaina truth.
Then the system tells us:

  best single generator = X

  best ensemble = X + Y



                                      🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                           31 / 188
  measured Elaina reconstruction gain = ...

  hallucination rate = ...

  UNKNOWN calibration = ...

  generic-personality contamination = ...


Only after that do we run the real 65 MC10 packets.
That is much more consistent with how seriously we're treating synthetic personality completion. We should not ask, “which Qwen
is generally smarter?” We should ask:

  Which generator, or combination of generators, most accurately predicts Elaina when we hide things about
  Elaina that we actually know?

That's the experiment that matters for Alice.

Yes. We start the qualification tournament now.
I do not want to choose the contestant set from assumptions about your machine. The tournament has to obey the
existing pseudo-gap doctrine: hidden E0 must be inaccessible to generator/retriever/prompt builder, candidate outputs must be
frozen before the evaluator sees gold, and one LLM judge is explicitly insufficient. ChatGPT-Memory Shadow Migration G(1).pdf MC8

holdouts, with candidate-visible and evaluator-only files physically separated. ChatGPT-Alice Memory Continuation.pdf ChatGPT-

The tournament will be staged like this:

  QUALIFICATION PREFLIGHT        ← NOW
          │
          ├─ exact MC8 bytes
          ├─ exact hardening policies
          ├─ current hardware/RAM
          ├─ free storage
          ├─ current Ollama runtime
          ├─ installed models

          ↓
  ROUND 1 - MODEL SCREEN
          │
          ├─ same pseudo-gaps
          ├─ same prompt
          ├─ same generation settings
          ├─ candidate never sees gold
          └─ multiple model families
          ↓
  ROUND 2 - SEMIFINAL
          │

  INDEPENDENT EVALUATION
          │
          ├─ deterministic gates
          ├─ hidden-E0 agreement
          ├─ UNKNOWN calibration
          ├─ contradiction / fabrication
          ├─ contextual fidelity
          ├─ flattening / genericness
          └─ independent judge evidence
          ↓

          ↓

Yep. This is another PowerShell-specific bug in my preflight wrapper, and it failed before touching the qualification
benchmark.
The problem is the function parameter name:

  param([string]$Exe,[string[]]$Args)


$Args is a built-in PowerShell automatic variable. In Windows PowerShell 5.1, using that name here caused the splat in:

  & $Exe @Args


to lose the intended argument array. So instead of receiving:

  git branch --show-current


Git effectively received just:

  git


which is why it printed its generic usage page and exited with code 1.
Nothing in MC8, MC10, the Vault, Ollama, or the tournament itself failed. We never got past STEP 0.
I patched only that collision and bumped the preflight to v1.0.1:

  [string[]]$Args    →   [string[]]$A
  @Args              →   @A


I also checked the full diff to make sure nothing else changed.

No - I would not settle for v16 yet. I found a real high-severity false-positive bug. The good news is that the
pipeline/output plumbing itself looks solid; this is a narrow semantic identity problem we can fix without redesigning the whole
matcher.
The core issue is national-brand variant collapse. BetterBasket explicitly says national-brand exact matches are the exact
same product, while the looser “essentially the same product” rule is for private-label/fresh non-exact matching. [BetterBasket]
Engineering Technical Assessment (1).pdf Your v16 is sometimes treating “same brand + same broad product family” as enough
to certify a national-brand match.
I found several indisputable examples in the final MATCH rows:

      Boiron is the clearest smoking gun. Store-B item 950203 , titled only Boiron Homeopathic Medicine, Unflavored, Meltaway
      Tablets , is simultaneously certified as the match for Boiron Arnicare pain tablets and Boiron Diaralia diarrhea tablets.
      Both are GPT_V16_STRUCTURED_MATCH / SAME_BRAND_PRODUCT . They obviously cannot both be the exact same Store-B SKU.
      4e88cb49-94f7-4438-9dfa-1a52828e93de.csv 4e88cb49-94f7-4438-9dfa-1a52828e93de.csv

      Mrs. Wages seasoning mix → finished pickles. Bread and Butter Pickle Mix ... 5.3 oz Pickle Seasoning Mix was
      certified against Mrs. Wages Pickles, Bread & Butter . The same file correctly rejects multipack versions elsewhere, which
      makes this single-pack acceptance even more inconsistent. 4e88cb49-94f7-4438-9dfa-1a52828e93de.csv
      Quaker Yellow Corn Meal → Quaker White Corn Meal. One copy is actually REVIEWed as a variant, while another
      essentially identical A listing gets promoted to MATCH by GPT. That is a particularly strong indication that the final decision
      logic is inconsistent. 4e88cb49-94f7-4438-9dfa-1a52828e93de.csv
      Banana Boat Baby SPF 50 → Banana Boat Kids Mineral SPF 50+. Different product variant, yet marked
      SAME_BRAND_PRODUCT . 4e88cb49-94f7-4438-9dfa-1a52828e93de.csv

      Wilton Pink Decorating Icing → Wilton White Decorating Icing. Even worse, the row itself contains an
      ATTRIBUTE_ASYMMETRY signal, yet GPT still finalized it as MATCH. 4e88cb49-94f7-4438-9dfa-1a52828e93de.csv

      The deterministic lane has the same weakness: a generic Cottonelle Wipes, Flushable B listing automatically accepts the
      explicitly Summer Fresh Scented A variant. 4e88cb49-94f7-4438-9dfa-1a52828e93de.csv

So this is not just GPT making one weird call. Both GPT_V16_STRUCTURED_MATCH and AGGRESSIVE_MATCH_LANE can make the same
conceptual mistake.

The actual v17 fix should be narrow
I would not touch retrieval, concurrency, batching, the GPT cap, or the general scoring architecture. The bug is
downstream.
Add one final National Brand Exact-Identity Gate after every proposed MATCH, whether it came from deterministic logic or
GPT:

  NATIONAL BRAND
       │
       ├── same brand? ── no → NON_MATCH
       │
       └── yes
            │
            ├── explicit variant conflict
            │      flavor / scent / color
            │      formulation / product line
            │      role / subtype
            │      size / count / concentration
            │
            │         → NON_MATCH
            │
            ├── A is specific but B is generic /
            │    decisive variant information absent
            │
            │         → REVIEW
            │
            └── positive variant identity established
                      → MATCH


The crucial change is:

  Missing variant information for a national-brand product must not behave like a wildcard.

Right now, Boiron Homeopathic Medicine... effectively means “any Boiron meltaway medicine.” It needs to mean “identity
unresolved” unless other B metadata proves which medicine it is.
We can also add a catalog-consistency check: when the same B SKU is being assigned to mutually incompatible A variants,
those assignments cannot all survive automatically. That would have caught the Boiron/Bounty/Cottonelle patterns immediately.
The assessment only requires ≥4,000 submitted matches, although it says the complete set is >10,000. [BetterBasket]
Engineering Technical Assessment (1).pdf We currently have 8,939, so we have room to become more conservative-but I would
run the guard first and see the real resulting count, rather than assuming how many remain.
Verdict: v16 is close structurally, but not submission-safe yet. I would make exactly one v17 identity-guard repair,
re-audit the resulting matches for false positives, and then settle. I would not start another broad recall rewrite.

Hidden E0 will never be sent to the remote worker. The remote side only gets the minimum candidate-visible pseudo-gap
packet necessary to generate a hypothesis. That also follows the handoff's requirement to stage only the data required for the
job. ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1.md
Anything Alice-specific sent remotely will have generic filenames and live only under the random /dev/shm/.rw-* job workspace.
Nothing sensitive goes in command-line arguments. Retained candidate output comes back through RW_OUTPUT , and the remote
copy then disappears. ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1.md ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1.md Every remote batch
must end with both residue-audit passes; otherwise the tournament stops using that worker.

                                      🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                         51 / 188
Current official Ollama packages put Qwen3.5-9B at 6.6 GB, 27B at 17 GB and 35B-A3B at 24 GB. (Ollama) The current Qwen3.6
equivalents are also roughly 17 GB for 27B and 24 GB for 35B-A3B. (Ollama) That makes the remote 31-GiB system especially
interesting: the Q4 large models are plausible contestants, although actual RAM/VRAM/runtime overhead still has to be
measured before declaring them viable.
And I definitely want Qwen3.6 to compete, not automatically win. Its public improvements emphasize agentic coding and
reasoning-context preservation, which are not the same thing as predicting Elaina. (Ollama) The MC8 pseudo-gap result decides
that.
Gemma 3 12B is ~8.1 GB, Mistral NeMo 12B ~7.1 GB, and Phi-4 14B ~9.1 GB, giving us genuine family diversity instead of a
Qwen beauty contest. (Ollama)


We should also exploit parallelism differently
I wouldn't simply launch five large models simultaneously on the RTX 3050. One GPU has only ~8 GiB VRAM; making
several large models fight over it can actually make the tournament slower and introduce uneven resource pressure.
Instead:

 WINDOWS
 Qwen3.5-9B / Qwen3-8B
         │
         │ simultaneously
         ▼
 REMOTE GPU + RAM
 one high-capacity model at a time
 27B / 35B-A3B / 14B / 12B


While the remote box works on a large-model tranche, the laptop works on a smaller-model tranche. Within an individual model
we can batch several pseudo-gaps where the backend supports it.
That gives us host-level parallelism without contaminating the quality comparison with resource starvation.
For the 35B-A3B Qwens there is another interesting reason to test rather than dismiss them by total parameter count: A3B
denotes a mixture-of-experts structure with roughly 3B parameters activated per token, rather than all 35B active per token.
Qwen's naming convention explicitly uses A for activated parameters in MoE models. (GitHub) Their 24-GB weight footprint is
still real, though, so we'll measure actual execution.


Tournament progression
Rather than running all 365 MC8 pseudo-gaps × nine models immediately, I'll make it elimination-based:

 ROUND 0 - runtime qualification
 public synthetic prompts only
 no Alice data
 → loadability, JSON compliance, speed, RAM/VRAM

 ROUND 1 - blinded screening
 \~30-50 stratified MC8 pseudo-gaps
 9 contestants
 → eliminate clearly inferior configurations

 ROUND 2 - semifinal
 larger held-out stratified set
 top \~4

 ROUND 3 - full qualification
 185 interaction-cluster holdouts
 +
 180 five-fold holdouts
 top \~2-3




                                      🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                        52 / 188
  FINAL
  single-model ranking
  +
  complementarity analysis

  → best single generator
  → best multi-model ensemble
  → freeze generator configuration
  → actual MC10B E-INF


This is important because the ensemble may beat the individual winner. If, for example, Qwen3.6-35B predicts
relationship/context behavior best while Gemma catches a different class of Elaina behavior and Qwen3.5 has better UNKNOWN
calibration, MC10 can deliberately use all three proposal sources instead of throwing away useful diversity.
Also, the existing MC10 design already expects four generation methods and multiple seeds. So eventually we can get:

  MODEL FAMILY diversity
          ×
  GENERATION METHOD diversity
          ×
  SEED diversity


instead of the old:

  one qwen3:8b
  × four methods
  × three seeds


That's a much stronger E-INF candidate-generation architecture.
One security decision I'm also making: no persistent Ollama/LLM server will ever receive Alice prompts on the remote
host. Public weights may persist there under your new authorization, but the actual inference process for Alice-sensitive work
should execute inside the hardened ephemeral job and read those weights read-only. That avoids introducing another persistent
daemon/logging surface.
And because changing alice-remote.ps1 or alice_remote_hardened.py would trigger mandatory security revalidation, I don't
intend to modify either just to accomplish this. The handoff explicitly lists launcher changes as revalidation triggers.
 ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1.md We'll make the tournament worker conform to the existing interface, not
weaken the interface for the tournament.
So yes: this compute upgrade changes my recommendation significantly. We should now let 27B/35B models compete,
use the laptop and RTX worker concurrently, and let MC8 decide both the best individual generator and the best heterogeneous
ensemble.
The next script I give you should therefore be a public-data-only dual-host runtime/model qualification stage, replacing
the previous contestant-preparation script. It will install/inventory the generic public models, benchmark what can actually run on
each host, and still expose zero MC8/Elaina content. Only after that passes do we open Round 1.

IDs now use a random local salt; same-family judges don't even receive those candidates; exact model/runtime digests are

 $Script = Join-Path $HOME "Downloads\Run-ALICEGeneratorQualificationTournamentV1-v1.0.0.ps1"

 if ($ActualHash -ne $ExpectedHash) {

 $Tokens = $null
 $ParseErrors = $null

 [System.Management.Automation.Language.Parser]::ParseFile(
     $Script,
     [ref]$Tokens,
     [ref]$ParseErrors
 ) | Out-Null

 if (@($ParseErrors).Count -gt 0) {
     $ParseErrors | Format-List Message, Extent
     throw "Parser check failed. Script was NOT executed."
 }

 Write-Host "Parser check passed."

 Unblock-File -LiteralPath $Script -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue

 & powershell.exe `

     -File $Script

The new repository rule is much stronger than simply ignoring untracked files:

  tracked Git files
      MUST be completely clean

  pre-existing untracked files
      ONLY allowed beneath:
          tools/
          remote-results/

  every allowed untracked file
      path + SHA-256 + byte size
      ↓
  frozen into baseline fingerprint

  TOURNAMENT RUNS

  final tracked Git state
      MUST still be clean

  final untracked fingerprint
      MUST exactly equal starting fingerprint


So an unrelated foo.txt , newly created repository artifact, changed tool, altered old remote result, etc. still fails closed. We are
merely distinguishing pre-existing operational state from an actual repository mutation.
I also did the deeper audit before handing you another script. Two useful findings:




                                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                     61 / 188
    The tournament already does not wastefully regenerate cumulative tasks. Round 1 evaluates 32 tasks; Round 2
    generates only the additional 64; the final generates only the remaining 269. Earlier candidate/judgment stores are carried
    forward for advancing models.
    I considered reducing the four-family judge quorum in early rounds to gain speed, but did not change it. The current
    implementation already batches judge work and gives us the strongest apples-to-apples cumulative final evaluation.
    Changing judge depth now would complicate cumulative qualification/top-up semantics for relatively little architectural
    benefit.

The local lane still operates concurrently with the large-model remote lane, while the large remote models remain sequential to
avoid making several 15-24 GB models fight over one 8-GB GPU and the same system RAM.

The work root already contained the older tournament config with hash C776... , while v1.0.2 expected the new remote-only
config 2346... . The script correctly refused to mix two tournament definitions, but it handled that situation badly: it stopped
instead of preserving the predecessor work package and starting the new configuration cleanly.
While fixing that, I found something more important in v1.0.2: the benchmark materializer was parsing the MC8 evaluator-only
gold before candidate generation, then storing a local evaluator_all_365.jsonl . It still never sent that material to generators,
so no leakage occurred, but that timing is weaker than the ratified rule that evaluator-only gold should become semantically
available only after candidate freeze. MC8's architecture specifically separates candidate-visible evidence from evaluator-only
hidden E0. ChatGPT-Alice Memory Continuation.pdf
Since your run stopped before Step 1, none of that evaluator materialization happened in this attempt.
I fixed both problems rather than just changing the expected hash.

What v1.0.3 changes
First, incompatible resumable state is now handled safely:

  existing .work
      │
      ├─ current config hash
      │      → resume
      │
      ├─ known predecessor hash
      │      → preserve whole directory
      │        as .preserved-superseded-config-...
      │      → create fresh v1.0.3 workroot
      │
      └─ unknown config hash
             → FAIL CLOSED


So your existing C776... package will not be deleted or rewritten. The controller recognizes both known predecessor hashes:

  C776C58D3144B9BF86BA6C20B19F43F73CAB3B6A00C7FE33FD8CFB95FC7B54E4
  2346C9431DB69EB64F81FBCF9F34F928231E083F3D74F6FC68D0E68BDD08FD0F


and preserves them before restarting.
Second, the MC8 firewall is now stronger:

  STEP 2
  candidate-view MC8 tasks only
           ↓
  visible-E0 prompt materialization
           ↓
  REMOTE GENERATION
           ↓
  candidate output returned locally
           ↓
  exact candidate bytes frozen
           ↓
  pre-evaluator freeze receipt written + verified
           ↓
  ONLY NOW
  MC8 evaluator-only gold is semantically parsed
           ↓
  blind IDs



                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                              69 / 188
          ↓
  independent judge inputs
          ↓
  REMOTE JUDGING


The hidden MC8 files are still hash-verified beforehand for byte integrity, but their semantic JSON content is not parsed into
evaluator records until after candidate freeze. That distinction matters.
I also caught an integrity hole: v1.0.2 consumed the MC8C/MC8D reconstruction-result files for stratified sampling without directly
pinning their hashes. v1.0.3 now pins them too:

  mc8c_interaction_cluster_holdout_results_v1.jsonl
  D7889D6BC50439D8A540528AD39946AFF6189FDBD4A210562030589F0C4E4B0B

  mc8d_interaction_five_fold_results_v1.jsonl
  BE8C73F193485DE73ECB6954238B5335A592156EBFDE0B84EE6BF026E22D4C62


Those are the exact hashes from the frozen MC8 builder.
I also hardened actual resumption. A generator is no longer considered complete just because some worker_summary.json
happens to exist. It now requires the candidate payload, matching model tag, matching exact model digest, and the expected
task count. Invalid partial model output is preserved under a sibling .preserved-* directory before recomputation. Judges get the
equivalent check.
The remote security model remains unchanged: sensitive jobs still use the hardened ephemeral worker, swap-disabled execution
and mandatory cleanup/residue verification. ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1.md A residue-audit failure still blocks further
sensitive dispatch. ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1.md
And local Ollama remains completely unused.
I also rechecked the unstable contestant tags against Ollama's current registry today. The important large contestants used by
the script are still valid: Qwen3.5-27B is 17 GB, Qwen3.6-27B is 17 GB, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B is 24 GB, Gemma3-27B-QAT is 18 GB,

                                         🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                 73 / 188

Realistically add ~20-60+ minutes afterward because the script then loads/smoke-tests all ten models, including 17-24 GB

will spill substantially into the remote system RAM; that qualification is deliberately measuring whether they are actually usable

  Round 1:
  10 models × 32 tasks
  = 320 generation calls

  Round 2:
  5 survivors × 64 NEW tasks
  = 320 generation calls

  Final:
  3 survivors × 269 NEW tasks
  = 807 generation calls

  TOTAL GENERATION
  = 1,447 model calls


Then every candidate gets a four-independent-family judging quorum. That's up to 5,788 candidate/judge evaluations,
although the judge worker packs several evaluations into each model call rather than making 5,788 separate API requests.
Because the remote has one GPU and we intentionally run the big models sequentially, I would budget about 18-40 hours for
the entire tournament after downloading if most of the big contestants survive. It could finish faster if the MoE models run
well or several large dense models fail the no-swap qualification; it could go beyond a day if the dense 27B models are
particularly slow with CPU/RAM offload.
So my practical expectation from now is:

  Fast case: ~12-18 hours
  Likely case: ~20-30 hours
  Slow but still plausible: ~36-48 hours

The good part is that the ~158 GB download is one-time. Those generic public weights persist remotely under your
authorization. If the tournament stops later and we rerun v1.0.3, it should reuse the downloaded models and its validated
completed tournament stages rather than starting all this over.
Also, don't close this PowerShell window. The current absence of output does not mean it froze. At this exact point it is very likely
pulling those models in the background.
If you want, I can also give you one separate PowerShell command you can run in another window to watch the remote
model-download progress live without interfering with this tournament.

 $Monitor = @'
 set +e

 BASE="$HOME/.local/share/model-lab-v1"
 MODELS="$BASE/models"

 while true; do
     printf '\033[2J\033[H'
     echo "======================================================================"
     echo " A.L.I.C.E. REMOTE TOURNAMENT MONITOR   $(date -Is)"
     echo "======================================================================"

     echo
     echo "===== GPU ====="
     nvidia-smi \
       --query-
 gpu=name,utilization.gpu,utilization.memory,memory.used,memory.free,memory.total,temperature.gpu,power.draw \
       --format=csv,noheader 2>/dev/null || echo "nvidia-smi unavailable"

      echo
      echo "===== SYSTEM RAM ====="
      free -h

      echo
      echo "===== LOAD ====="
      uptime

      echo
      echo "===== PERSISTENT PUBLIC MODEL STORE ====="
      if [ -d "$MODELS" ]; then
           printf "model_store_size="
           du -sh "$MODELS" 2>/dev/null | awk '{print $1}'

       echo
       echo "-- Installed model manifests --"
       find "$MODELS/manifests" -type f -printf '%P\n' 2>/dev/null | sort
else
       echo "model store not created yet"
fi

echo
echo "===== OLLAMA RUNTIME ====="

     "$BASE/runtime/bin/ollama" -v 2>&1 | tail -1

else
     echo "Ollama runtime not installed yet"

FOUND=0
for D in /tmp/model-lab-status-*; do
     [ -d "$D" ] || continue
     FOUND=1

       for F in "$D"/*.log; do
           [ -f "$F" ] || continue
           N="$(basename "$F" .log)"
           S="${F%.log}.status"

              echo

              if [ -f "$S" ]; then
                  echo "pull_exit=$(cat "$S" 2>/dev/null)"
              else
                  echo "pull_status=RUNNING_OR_WAITING"
              fi

              # Ollama uses carriage-return progress lines.
              # Convert them only for display. No file is modified.
              tr '\r' '\n' < "$F" 2>/dev/null |
                  sed '/^[[:space:]]*$/d' |
                  tail -n 5
       done
done

if [ "$FOUND" -eq 0 ]; then
    echo "No public pull-status directory active."
fi

echo
echo "===== RUNTIME / ARCHIVE DOWNLOAD ====="
ls -lh /tmp/model-runtime.* 2>/dev/null || echo "No runtime archive currently downloading."

              grep -E \

              head -40

         echo "===== TOP CPU / RAM PROCESSES ====="
         ps -eo pid,etimes,%cpu,%mem,rss,comm --sort=-%cpu | head -12

What you'll be able to see
During the phase you're in right now, pay most attention to:

  ===== ACTIVE PUBLIC MODEL DOWNLOADS =====


You'll see something like:

  --- qwen3_8b ---
  pull_status=RUNNING_OR_WAITING

                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                           77 / 188
The tournament provisioner intentionally launches two model pulls at a time, so normally two logs should be actively
advancing. The generic public model store is:

  /home/newuser/.local/share/model-lab-v1/models


and the runtime is:

  /home/newuser/.local/share/model-lab-v1/runtime


Those are precisely the generic persistent public-model resources authorized for this competition; the actual A.L.I.C.E. state
remains local/canonical. The remote handoff defines the worker as compute-only for A.L.I.C.E. state.
ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1.md

Later, once model downloads finish and the real tournament begins, watch:

  ===== HARDENED EPHEMERAL WORKSPACES =====


You should periodically see:

  /dev/shm/.rw-<random-token>      <size>
  active_rw_workspaces=1


That means a hardened generation/judging job is alive in RAM. When that job ends, the .rw-* workspace should disappear. The
architecture explicitly requires those transient workspaces to be removed after execution.
ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1.md

The GPU section will show whether a model is actually exercising the RTX 3050:

  NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3050 OEM, 87 %, ..., 6742 MiB, ...


For the 17-24 GB models, don't be surprised if GPU memory is near full while system RAM usage also jumps considerably. That
means Ollama is offloading only part of the model to the 8-GiB GPU.
One deliberate omission: this monitor never prints the contents of /dev/shm/.rw-* , tournament prompts, MC8
evidence, candidate outputs, or judge payloads. It monitors processes, resource consumption, workspace existence/size,
and the public model-download logs only. That lets you watch essentially everything operational without turning the monitor itself
into another private-data exposure surface.
And yes: Ctrl+C in this second window only kills this monitoring SSH connection. It does not touch the tournament
running in your first PowerShell window.

  )

  ssh alice-gpu @'
  set -e

  OLD_ROOT=1824057
  OLD_OLLAMA=1824659
  NEW_ROOT=1857134
  NEW_OLLAMA=1857156

  echo "===== SAFETY CHECK ====="

  OLD_CMD="$(ps -p "$OLD_ROOT" -o cmd= || true)"
  NEW_CMD="$(ps -p "$NEW_ROOT" -o cmd= || true)"
  NEW_OLLAMA_CMD="$(ps -p "$NEW_OLLAMA" -o cmd= || true)"

  echo "old_root=$OLD_ROOT cmd=$OLD_CMD"
  echo "new_root=$NEW_ROOT cmd=$NEW_CMD"
  echo "new_ollama=$NEW_OLLAMA cmd=$NEW_OLLAMA_CMD"

    echo "REFUSING: old root no longer matches expected process."

    echo "REFUSING: current v1.0.3 root is not alive."
    exit 1
}

echo
echo "===== VERIFY OLD/NEW PROVISIONER SIGNATURES ====="

OLD_SIG="$(ps -eo pid,ppid,args |
    awk '$2 == 1824057 || $2 == 1886206 {print}' |
    grep 'anaconda3/bin/python' || true)"

NEW_SIG="$(ps -eo pid,ppid,args |
    awk '$2 == 1857134 || $2 == 1886305 {print}' |
    grep 'anaconda3/bin/python' || true)"

echo "$OLD_SIG"

echo "-- current --"
echo "$NEW_SIG"

echo "$NEW_SIG" | grep -q 'qwen3:8b' || {
    echo "REFUSING: expected remote-only v1.0.3 signature not found."
    exit 1
}

kill_tree() {
    P="$1"

    for C in $(pgrep -P "$P" 2>/dev/null || true); do
         kill_tree "$C"
    done

    kill -TERM "$P" 2>/dev/null || true
}

def alive(pid):
    return Path(f"/proc/{pid}").exists()



def cmd(pid):
    try:
         b = Path(f"/proc/{pid}/cmdline").read_bytes()
         return b.replace(b"\0", b" ").decode("utf-8", "replace").strip()
    except Exception:
         return ""



def parent(pid):
    try:
         for line in Path(f"/proc/{pid}/status").read_text().splitlines():
              if line.startswith("PPid:"):
                  return int(line.split()[1])
    except Exception:
         pass
    return None



def process_map():
    children = {}
    for p in Path("/proc").iterdir():
        if not p.name.isdigit():
            continue
        pid = int(p.name)
        pp = parent(pid)
        if pp is not None:
            children.setdefault(pp, []).append(pid)
    return children



def descendants(root):
    ch = process_map()
    out = []

    def walk(p):
        for c in ch.get(p, []):
            walk(c)
            out.append(c)

    walk(root)
    return out



print("===== SAFETY CHECK =====")

old_cmd = cmd(OLD_ROOT)
new_cmd = cmd(NEW_ROOT)
old_ollama_cmd = cmd(OLD_OLLAMA)
new_ollama_cmd = cmd(NEW_OLLAMA)

print(f"old_root={OLD_ROOT} cmd={old_cmd}")
print(f"old_ollama={OLD_OLLAMA} cmd={old_ollama_cmd}")
print(f"new_root={NEW_ROOT} cmd={new_cmd}")
print(f"new_ollama={NEW_OLLAMA} cmd={new_ollama_cmd}")

if old_cmd != "bash -s":

    raise SystemExit(
        f"REFUSING: stale root identity changed: {old_cmd!r}"
    )

if new_cmd != "bash -s":
    raise SystemExit(
        f"REFUSING: current v1.0.3 root identity changed: {new_cmd!r}"
    )

if parent(OLD_OLLAMA) != OLD_ROOT:
    raise SystemExit(
        f"REFUSING: old Ollama is no longer child of {OLD_ROOT}"
    )

if parent(NEW_OLLAMA) != NEW_ROOT:
    raise SystemExit(
        f"REFUSING: current Ollama is no longer child of {NEW_ROOT}"
    )

if not old_ollama_cmd.endswith("/ollama serve"):
    raise SystemExit(
        f"REFUSING: unexpected stale Ollama command: {old_ollama_cmd!r}"
    )

if not new_ollama_cmd.endswith("/ollama serve"):
    raise SystemExit(
        f"REFUSING: unexpected current Ollama command: {new_ollama_cmd!r}"
    )

old_tree = descendants(OLD_ROOT) + [OLD_ROOT]
new_tree = set(descendants(NEW_ROOT) + [NEW_ROOT])

overlap = set(old_tree) & new_tree
if overlap:
    raise SystemExit(
        f"REFUSING: old/current process trees overlap: {sorted(overlap)}"
    )

print(f"stale_tree_processes={len(old_tree)}")
print(f"current_tree_processes={len(new_tree)}")
print("safety_check_passed=true")



print()

# descendants() is already bottom-up.

  ✓ Worked for 5m 18s


Yes. We should fix this before the next tournament run, and the right requirement is slightly different from “SSH should never
die”:

  SSH is allowed to die. The remote job must not die with it.

A normal SSH/TCP connection cannot reliably survive changing Wi-Fi/IP addresses or being completely offline for 10-20 minutes.
Keepalives alone cannot solve that. Instead, the controller should detect the disconnect quickly, let the remote job continue
independently, then reattach when your laptop gets internet again.
The timing is good because v1.0.3 has already stopped before any private MC8 benchmark opened. Its hardened job
itself succeeded with zero swap/payload residue, but the tournament falsely rejected it afterward. Pasted text.txt The actual
stop was the controller's missing-signal check, not a remote security failure. Pasted text.txt I also traced that bug: the wrapper


                                            🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                      105 / 188
emits its final acceptance through Write-Host , while v1.0.3 only captured stdout/stderr; the successor will capture all PowerShell
streams.

The network-safe design
I'm going to make the next remote layer work like this:

  WINDOWS - canonical state
          │
          │ short SSH connection
          ▼
  1. Stage complete input into remote RAM
     /dev/shm/.rw-<random-token>
          │
          │ verify complete input hash
          ▼
  2. Launch DETACHED hardened systemd job
     MemorySwapMax=0
     remote disk read-only
          │
          ├──────── SSH may disappear here ────────┐
          │                                          │
          ▼                                          │
  3. Job keeps computing on remote PC              │
     independent of laptop/network                   │
          │                                          │
          ▼                                          │
  4. Result finishes into RAM                        │
     state = WAITING_FOR_RETRIEVAL                     │
          │                                          │
          │ bounded \~90-minute result lease            │
          │                                          │
          ◄──────── laptop reconnects ───────────────┘
          │
  5. Windows asks about SAME job token
          │
          ▼
  6. Result transferred to local TEMP
          │
          ▼
  7. SHA-256 + schema + expected-job identity verified
          │
          ▼
  8. Atomic local publish
          │
          ▼
  9. Windows sends ACK
          │
          ▼
  10. Remote deletes RAM workspace
          │
          ▼
  11. Mandatory residue audit
          │
          ▼
  12. only then is result accepted


The current security guarantees remain intact: sensitive jobs remain in /dev/shm , the cgroup remains MemorySwapMax=0 , and
persistent remote disk remains read-only. ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1.md The existing architecture already requires
zero workspace/payload/cache/swap residue on both successful and failed jobs; we're extending that rule to client/network
failure too. ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1(1).md



                                       🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                         106 / 188
The failure behavior will be deterministic:

    Offline before dispatch: Windows simply waits. Nothing gets staged remotely.
    Wi-Fi dies during staging: compute never starts from a partial package. A remote pre-launch guardian expires the
    incomplete RAM workspace and audits it.
    Wi-Fi dies during computation: detached systemd computation continues normally. Your laptop can be offline 20 minutes
    or sleep.
    Wi-Fi dies after computation: completed result waits in /dev/shm under a bounded lease. It is not written to remote disk.
    Wi-Fi dies during download: Windows does not ACK it. On reconnect it downloads the same result again and verifies the
    digest.
    Laptop reboots: the local tournament WorkRoot remembers the outstanding random job token. On restart it reconciles that
    token before creating another job.
    You remain offline beyond the result lease: remote automatically destroys the result/workspace and audits itself. On
    reconnect, Windows sees the job as expired and safely recomputes from canonical local inputs.
    Remote PC reboots: RAM state disappears. Windows detects the missing outstanding job and reruns from local canonical
    state.
    Cleanup/audit fails: no automatic sensitive retry. Remote dispatch becomes blocked until we inspect it.

Critically, the remote supervisor-not your laptop-will own the cleanup timer. Otherwise a network outage could strand sensitive
/dev/shm state.

The current remote system already uses a transient systemd user cgroup and a cleanup path, but its lifecycle is still driven by the
local launcher. ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1(1).md That's the part we need to upgrade.


What v1.0.4 will also fix
I'm going to combine everything into one successor, rather than having you run another intermediate tournament:

  remote compute v2.2
  +
  tournament v1.0.4


v1.0.4 will:

  ✓ use network-resilient detached jobs
  ✓ survive Wi-Fi/IP changes and 10-20 min offline periods
  ✓ wait/reconnect automatically instead of failing the tournament
  ✓ reconcile uncertain in-flight jobs before retrying
  ✓ never create duplicate jobs just because SSH disappeared
  ✓ preserve canonical progress locally after every completed batch
  ✓ fix v1.0.3's Write-Host/all-stream acceptance bug
  ✓ preserve the contaminated v1.0.3 public-smoke receipt as history
  ✓ rerun public model qualification cleanly
  ✓ reuse all 149 GB of already-downloaded model weights
  ✓ NOT redownload the models
  ✓ retain the 5-independent-judge-family requirement
  ✓ prevent private MC8 from opening before remote v2.2 is revalidated


And I'm not going to rely on autossh , giant SSH keepalive values, tmux , or a persistent remote Alice directory. Those can make a
terminal more convenient, but they don't give us the exact recovery/security semantics we need. The existing handoff explicitly
prohibits persistent remote A.L.I.C.E. state and requires the wrapper path plus residue auditing.
ALICE_REMOTE_SSH_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v2.1(1).md

For SSH itself we'll actually prefer quick failure detection + automatic reconnect, rather than pretending a dead TCP
connection is alive for 20 minutes.

 $Src = "C:\A.L.I.C.E-main\tools"
 $Dst = Join-Path $HOME "Downloads\alice-remote-current-source"

 if (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Dst) {
     throw "Destination already exists: $Dst"
 }

 New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path $Dst | Out-Null

 $Files = @(
     "alice-remote.ps1",
     "alice_remote_hardened.py"
 )

 foreach ($Name in $Files) {
     $Source = Join-Path $Src $Name

      if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Source -PathType Leaf)) {
          throw "Missing source file: $Source"
      }

      Copy-Item `
          -LiteralPath $Source `
          -Destination (Join-Path $Dst $Name)
 }

 $Hashes = foreach ($Name in $Files) {
     $P = Join-Path $Dst $Name
     $H = Get-FileHash -LiteralPath $P -Algorithm SHA256

      [pscustomobject]@{
          File   = $Name
          SHA256 = $H.Hash.ToUpperInvariant()
          Bytes = (Get-Item -LiteralPath $P).Length
      }
 }

 $Hashes | Format-Table -AutoSize

 $Hashes |
     ConvertTo-Json |
     Set-Content `
         -LiteralPath (Join-Path $Dst "SHA256.json") `
         -Encoding UTF8

 $Zip = Join-Path $HOME "Downloads\alice-remote-current-source.zip"

 if (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Zip) {
     throw "Archive already exists: $Zip"
 }

 Compress-Archive `
     -Path (Join-Path $Dst "*") `
     -DestinationPath $Zip

After downloading the ZIP, run this exact block:

  $Zip = Join-Path $HOME "Downloads\ALICE_Remote_v2.2_Tournament_v1.0.4.zip"
  $ExpectedZipHash = "928672FC3FCCBD8418739A1CCB272B8908E7F9876014A248A9A653830FEE23D8"

  if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Zip -PathType Leaf)) {
      throw "Bundle not found: $Zip"
  }

  $ActualZipHash = (
      Get-FileHash -LiteralPath $Zip -Algorithm SHA256
  ).Hash.ToUpperInvariant()

  if ($ActualZipHash -ne $ExpectedZipHash) {
      throw "Bundle SHA-256 mismatch. Expected=$ExpectedZipHash Actual=$ActualZipHash"
  }

  $Dest = Join-Path $HOME "Downloads\ALICE_Remote_v2.2_Tournament_v1.0.4"

  if (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Dest) {
      throw "Extraction destination already exists: $Dest"
  }




                                       🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                        111 / 188
  Expand-Archive `
      -LiteralPath $Zip `
      -DestinationPath (Join-Path $HOME "Downloads")

  $Script = Join-Path $Dest "Start-ALICERemoteV2_2AndTournamentV1_0_4.ps1"
  $ExpectedScriptHash = "FA5E8861D4EEC125FCB15FD2D9C834FFE070DB8033B1FDE00192968B43F8AB0B"

  $ActualScriptHash = (
      Get-FileHash -LiteralPath $Script -Algorithm SHA256
  ).Hash.ToUpperInvariant()

  if ($ActualScriptHash -ne $ExpectedScriptHash) {
      throw "Installer SHA-256 mismatch. Expected=$ExpectedScriptHash Actual=$ActualScriptHash"
  }

  $Tokens = $null
  $ParseErrors = $null

  [System.Management.Automation.Language.Parser]::ParseFile(
      $Script,
      [ref]$Tokens,
      [ref]$ParseErrors
  ) | Out-Null

  if (@($ParseErrors).Count -gt 0) {
      $ParseErrors | Format-List Message, Extent
      throw "Installer parser check failed. Nothing was executed."
  }

  Unblock-File -LiteralPath $Script -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue

  & powershell.exe `
      -NoProfile `
      -ExecutionPolicy Bypass `
      -File $Script

  if ($LASTEXITCODE -ne 0) {
      throw "v2.2 installer/tournament controller exited with code $LASTEXITCODE"
  }

v1.0.5
Download the v1.0.5 hotfix bundle

Bundle SHA-256:

  DDBCA7BFD95D34AC1719BD9DC10AF4A9AB2332549ED2ED09FCC0E629EDC7EAEC


v1.0.5 specifically does four important things:

1. It creates the temporary rw-project\tools and copies the exact already-installed v2.2 launcher there, then verifies its
   hash before using it.
2. It preserves the failed v1.0.4 workroot as history instead of deleting/reusing it.
3. It treats the old public model inventory as audit-only and performs fresh public runtime smokes. The ~149 GB generic model
   store remains in place, so the model blobs can be reused rather than intentionally redownloaded.
4. The public bootstrap now has a singleton flock , plus SSH exit-255 reconnect handling for 45 minutes. So a Wi-Fi switch
   cannot create two concurrent public provisioners again. Once we enter hardened jobs, the v2.2 detached/resumable job
   protocol takes over.

The core competition logic and remote worker themselves remain byte-identical:

  }

  }

      -File $Start

  if ($LASTEXITCODE -ne 0) {

Permanent fix: put both machines on Tailscale
This is a much better fit for A.L.I.C.E. than relying on the lab's LAN IP.
Tailscale gives each device a stable private overlay address, generally 100.x.y.z , that stays the same when you move between
home Wi-Fi, lab Wi-Fi, cellular, etc. It also handles NAT traversal and can relay encrypted traffic when a direct connection isn't
possible. (Tailscale)
The architecture then becomes:

  Your Windows laptop
  home Wi-Fi / lab Wi-Fi / hotspot
          │
          │ encrypted Tailscale network
          ▼
  stable 100.x.x.x / MagicDNS
          │
          ▼
  Ubuntu RTX 3050 worker
  lab/home/etc.
          │
          ▼
  normal OpenSSH :22
          │
          ▼
  A.L.I.C.E. remote v2.2


Importantly, we do not need to enable Tailscale SSH. We'll use Tailscale only as the network layer and keep your existing
OpenSSH key authentication and hardened A.L.I.C.E. wrapper unchanged.


When you're next able to reach the remote PC

                                       🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                     126 / 188
One design point changes for Kaggle
The old SSH system maintained ~158 GB of generic model weights on the remote PC.
We should not blindly replicate that architecture in Kaggle.
The new backend has two T4s with independent 16-GB VRAM spaces, and the handoff explicitly warns that 2 × 16 GB ≠ one 32 GB
GPU . Multi-GPU use has to be deliberate. ALICE_KAGGLE_REMOTE_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v0.2.md

Also, Kaggle has GPU quota, session limits, queue delays, disk/RAM constraints and kernel lifecycle constraints that we need to
respect. ALICE_KAGGLE_REMOTE_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v0.2.md
So the Kaggle tournament controller should probably use model-scoped/sharded jobs rather than provisioning all ten
models into one giant persistent remote model lab. That also fits Kaggle's natural private-Dataset/private-kernel lifecycle
much better.
The leakage firewall still matters even though Kaggle itself is trusted:

  GENERATOR KERNEL
  receives candidate-visible MC8 only
          ↓
  candidate outputs return to Windows
          ↓
  Windows freezes + hashes them
          ↓
  ONLY THEN
          ↓
  evaluator/judge payload gets built
          ↓
  JUDGE KERNEL


Trusted infrastructure does not mean we weaken experimental blinding.

  Set-Location C:\A.L.I.C.E-main

  $SourceRoot = "C:\A.L.I.C.E-main\tools"
  $Out = Join-Path $HOME "Downloads\alice-kaggle-v0.2-current-source"

  if (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Out) {
      throw "Destination already exists: $Out"
  }

  New-Item -ItemType Directory -Path $Out | Out-Null

  $Names = @(
      "alice_kaggle_v0.py",
      "alice-kaggle.ps1",
      "kaggle-smoke-test.py"
  )

  foreach ($Name in $Names) {
      $Source = Join-Path $SourceRoot $Name

      if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Source -PathType Leaf)) {
          throw "Missing active Kaggle file: $Source"
      }

      Copy-Item `
          -LiteralPath $Source `
          -Destination (Join-Path $Out $Name)
  }

  $Hashes = foreach ($Name in $Names) {
      $Path = Join-Path $Out $Name

      [pscustomobject]@{
          File    = $Name
          SHA256 = (
               Get-FileHash `
                   -LiteralPath $Path `
                   -Algorithm SHA256
          ).Hash.ToUpperInvariant()
          Bytes = (Get-Item -LiteralPath $Path).Length
      }
  }

      ConvertTo-Json |
      Set-Content `

          -Encoding UTF8

  $Zip = Join-Path $HOME "Downloads\alice-kaggle-v0.2-current-source.zip"

  if (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Zip) {
      throw "Archive already exists: $Zip"
  }

  Compress-Archive `
      -Path (Join-Path $Out "*") `
      -DestinationPath $Zip

  Write-Host ""
  Write-Host "UPLOAD:"
  Write-Host $Zip

scratch per stage and removed between models.


Network/commute resilience
This part is now built specifically for how you work.
A Kaggle kernel does not depend on your laptop maintaining a live SSH connection. The controller assigns each remote request a
deterministic hash-derived Dataset/kernel identity and writes local resume state before remote creation.


                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                   132 / 188
If you switch Wi-Fi or lose internet for 10-20 minutes:

  your laptop offline
          X
          X
  Kaggle kernel keeps running
          │
          ▼
  local controller retries Kaggle CLI
          │
  internet returns
          ▼
  same Dataset/kernel identity
          │
          ▼
  download result
          │
          ▼
  verify locally
          │
          ▼
  clean remote resources


If the disconnect exceeds the default 45-minute reconnect window, it exits with:

  75


and explicitly preserves the same remote job/state rather than marking the model failed or creating a second job.
Then you reconnect and rerun the same starter. It reconciles the existing deterministic Kaggle job first.
I also built and ran a fake-Kaggle integration test specifically for this. I simulated losing connectivity immediately after kernel
push:

  first controller:
  kernel pushed
  network disappears
  → exit 75
  → remote identity preserved

  second controller:
  same request
  → finds same kernel
  → does NOT push second kernel
  → waits for COMPLETE
  → downloads result
  → verifies job identity
  → deletes remote resources


The test confirmed:

  FAKE_KAGGLE_RESUME_TEST_PASS
  kernel_push_count = 1


So this isn't only conceptual retry logic.
The Kaggle handoff also explicitly allows the trusted backend to handle private A.L.I.C.E. material and retires the old SSH-specific
RAM-only/no-swap/zero-residue restrictions. ALICE_KAGGLE_REMOTE_COMPUTE_HANDOFF_v0.2.md


Long-running stage resilience
There is also model-level checkpointing.



                                         🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                          133 / 188
After every completed model, Kaggle writes an atomic model directory plus stage_progress.json .
If a 12-hour session or platform issue interrupts something like:

  final:
  model A ✅
  model B ✅
  model C halfway   ❌
Windows validates and keeps A+B.
The next attempt runs C only.
For final-generation specifically, I went further and limited execution to one finalist per Kaggle kernel, because 269 fresh tasks
on a large model is exactly where a single giant all-model session would unnecessarily risk the platform runtime ceiling.
