Yes. I did a fresh sweep as of today, August 23, 2026, and I would not start the current 10-model bundle yet.
The current roster was good when we designed it, but it has become stale surprisingly fast. Several serious ~20-35B open-weight
models either launched very recently or were missing from our first sweep. In particular, Qwen3.8-27B, Muse Glimmer 30B,
and Nemotron 3.5 Lightning landed in August, and Gemma 4 is a clear upgrade over the Gemma 3 model currently in our
pool. (GitHub)


The biggest misses
Model                                                                  Why it matters for our task

Qwen3.8-27B                                                            Mandatory. Released Aug 14. Officially scores 79.5 IFBench, 89.2 GPQA
                                                                       and 30.8 HLE versus 69.1 / 87.8 / 24.0 for Qwen3.6-27B. Ollama's local
                                                                       Q4 is only ~18 GB with 256K context. (Hugging Face)

Gemma 4 31B                                                            Mandatory. Our current Gemma 3 27B is obsolete for this tournament.
                                                                       Gemma 4 31B jumps from Gemma 3's 67.6→85.2 MMLU-Pro and
                                                                       42.4→84.3 GPQA, has native system-prompt support, and is ~20 GB Q4.
                                                                       (Ollama)

Muse Glimmer 30B                                                       Mandatory. Released Aug 10. Particularly interesting for us because it
                                                                       emphasizes precise instruction following, multi-step reasoning and long-
                                                                       running agent behavior; IFBench is 77.0. Local Q4 is ~18 GB. (Hugging
                                                                       Face)

GLM-4.7-Flash 30B-A3B                                                  Strong addition. Completely different model family, ~19 GB Q4, 198K
                                                                       context, with 75.2 GPQA, 14.4 HLE and 79.5 τ²-Bench. That diversity is
                                                                       scientifically valuable. (Ollama)

Nemotron 3.5 Lightning 30B-A3B                                         Worth testing. Released Aug 11 and only just became broadly available
                                                                       in Ollama. ~25 GB Q4, 3B active, designed for contextualized long-
                                                                       running agents. It is not obviously superior to Cascade-2 on every
                                                                       metric, which is exactly why it belongs in Round 1 rather than being
                                                                       assumed better. (Hugging Face)

OLMo 3.1 32B Instruct                                                  Not brand-new, but we missed it. ~19 GB Q4, strong instruction/chat
                                                                       model, and-more importantly-adds an independent Ai2 model family
                                                                       instead of giving half the tournament to Qwen variants. (Allen Institute
                                                                       for AI)


There is another important signal: independent/local-user evidence shows that the models specialize differently. Qwen3.8 is
currently much stronger overall than Muse on general intelligence, while Muse can be unusually good at instruction adherence;
Gemma 4 is also regarded as particularly strong for writing/personality-style tasks. (Artificial Analysis) That diversity is useful for
Elaina hypothesis generation because the best coding benchmark model is not automatically the best behavioral-
inference model.

I would change the design slightly: 12 candidates, not 10
For this specific experiment, forcing an arbitrary top 10 is actually worse science.
Our hidden MC8 benchmark is the thing that should determine which generator is best at:

  visible Elaina evidence
          ↓
  infer missing behavior
          ↓


                                           🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                  138 / 188
  hidden real Elaina E0
          ↓
  did the model reconstruct her correctly?


Public MMLU/coding/math scores are only screening evidence. They cannot tell us which model is best at reconstructing a
person's conditional behavior, contradictions, relationships, emotional responses and uncertainty.
So I would use 12 Round-1 contestants, then preserve our existing:

  12 models × 32 tasks
          ↓
  top 5

  top 5 × next 64 tasks
          ↓
  top 3

  top 3 × remaining 269
          ↓
  full 365-task evidence


Going from 10 → 12 only changes Round 1 from:

  320 generator calls


to:

  384 generator calls


Just 64 additional generation calls. The expensive later rounds still have only 5 and then 3 models. That is a very small cost
for substantially reducing the chance that we accidentally exclude the best generator before testing it.


Revised 12-model pool
I would now use:

  1.   qwen3.8:27b                 NEW
  2.   qwen3.6:27b
  3.   qwen3.6:35b-a3b

  4.   gemma4:31b                 NEW
  5.   muse-glimmer:30b           NEW

  6.   glm-4.7-flash              NEW

  7.   mistral-small3.2:24b

  8.   gpt-oss:20b

  9.   granite4.1:30b

  10. nemotron-cascade-2:30b
  11. nemotron-3.5-lightning:30b NEW

  12. olmo-3.1:32b-instruct       NEW TO OUR POOL


And I would remove:

  qwen3:8b
  qwen3.5:9b




                                         🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                    139 / 188
  qwen3.5:27b
  gemma3:27b-it-qat


The first three give us too much same-family redundancy now that Qwen3.8 exists. And Gemma 3 27B has been very
substantially superseded by Gemma 4 31B. Google's own numbers show the scale of that gap. (Hugging Face)
I would keep both Qwen3.6-27B and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B despite Qwen3.8. Qwen3.8 is stronger overall, but community
testing still reports meaningful stylistic/creative differences between 3.6 and newer models. Since our target is personality
reconstruction rather than SWE-bench, we should let hidden Elaina E0 decide whether those older variants contain useful
complementary behavior. (Reddit)


Models I checked but would not add
I also looked at some other recent releases.
Laguna XS 2.1 33B is impressive-70.9 SWE-bench Verified and 63.1 multilingual-but it is explicitly optimized for agentic
coding/long-horizon software work. For a limited first-round budget, it is less directly relevant than OLMo/GLM/Muse for behavioral
inference. (Hugging Face)
New huge models such as DeepSeek V4 Flash, Kimi K3, Mistral Small 4 119B, Mistral Medium 3.5 128B, Nemotron Super and
similar models are either cloud-oriented or too large for the practical 2×T4 Kaggle qualification regime we established. Ollama
currently exposes DeepSeek V4 Flash and Kimi K3 as cloud-class entries rather than normal ~30B local candidates. (Ollama)
And I wouldn't replace Cascade-2 with Nemotron 3.5 Lightning blindly. Cascade-2 actually has excellent instruction/creative-
writing figures in NVIDIA's published evaluation-82.9 IFBench and 78.7 creative-writing ArenaHard-while Lightning is optimized
more heavily around efficient always-on agent execution. (Hugging Face) Run both and let MC8 decide.

One more thing I caught
Because these models differ considerably, our successor shouldn't just change model names.
We should give each model an explicit inference profile:

  model
  ├── sampling parameters
  ├── reasoning mode
  ├── context size
  ├── structured-output capability
  ├── JSON validation
  ├── timeout
  └── retry policy


For example, Qwen3.8 supports multiple reasoning-effort levels, and there is already an open report that its default xhigh mode
can occasionally terminate without an answer. (GitHub) We should not unknowingly handicap it-or any competitor-by imposing
one generic inference configuration across every architecture.
That does not mean tuning models against hidden MC8. Model profiles must be frozen before private MC8 opens, using only
public smoke/structured-output tests.
So the clean conclusion is:
No-the existing 10 are no longer the best candidate pool.
I would not run the Kaggle tournament v1.0.0 we just made. The public model landscape changed enough that we caught
this at exactly the right time.
The scientifically stronger final setup is:
12 generators, 8-ish independent judge families, model-specific frozen inference profiles, same MC8 hidden-E0
benchmark, same top-5 → top-3 elimination, and zero reuse of the old SSH tournament evidence.
That is the roster I would lock before we spend the Kaggle quota on the real competition.

The planned judge pool has 9 distinct model families. The tournament still requires at least 5 viable judge families before
private MC8 may open, and every candidate gets four independent non-generator-family judges.
I also caught and fixed an important fairness issue during the last code audit: the old public smoke allowed only 192 output
tokens. A thinking model could spend those tokens reasoning and be falsely declared nonviable. The locked version now gives
thinking-profile smoke tests 1,024 tokens and full schema/profile tests 4,096 tokens. Actual private generation also gets 4,096
output tokens. GPT-OSS receives its proper low/medium/high reasoning settings rather than a Boolean because Ollama explicitly
requires that distinction. (Ollama)
The non-thinking Mistral/Granite/OLMo instruct profiles now omit the thinking parameter entirely, rather than forcing
think:false .


Final methodological check
We are on the right track.
The tournament does not create E-INF. It is only determining which external stateless proposal generator best reconstructs held-
out Elaina behavior. That boundary was explicit before the tournament was designed. Pasted text(20260814-083256).txt
The MC8 firewall is unchanged: generator payloads contain only candidate-visible E0; candidate bytes must be frozen first;
evaluator-only hidden E0 is opened only afterward. ChatGPT-Alice Memory Continuation.pdf
The actual qualification core remains byte-identical to the approved version:

Commands
1. First-time launch
Download the ZIP above into your normal Windows Downloads folder. Then run this whole block:

 Set-Location C:\A.L.I.C.E-main

 $ErrorActionPreference = "Stop"

 $Zip = Join-Path `
     $HOME `
     "Downloads\ALICE_Kaggle_Tournament_LOCKED12_v1.1.0.zip"

 $ExpectedZipHash = `
     "D81B68C941EEC042B2AC90D84AF73E45762B6DD9658ADE870A2D04C983AAA77C"

 if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Zip -PathType Leaf)) {
     throw "Tournament ZIP not found: $Zip"
 }

 $ActualZipHash = (
     Get-FileHash `
         -LiteralPath $Zip `
         -Algorithm SHA256
 ).Hash.ToUpperInvariant()

 Write-Host "zip_sha256=$ActualZipHash"

 if ($ActualZipHash -ne $ExpectedZipHash) {
     throw "ZIP SHA-256 mismatch. Expected=$ExpectedZipHash Actual=$ActualZipHash"
 }

 Write-Host "zip_hash_verified=true"

 $Downloads = Join-Path $HOME "Downloads"

 $BundleRoot = Join-Path `
     $Downloads `
     "ALICE_Kaggle_Tournament_LOCKED12_v1.1.0"

 if (Test-Path -LiteralPath $BundleRoot) {
     throw "Extraction destination already exists: $BundleRoot"
 }

 Expand-Archive `
     -LiteralPath $Zip `
     -DestinationPath $Downloads

$Starter = Join-Path `
    $BundleRoot `
    "Start-ALICEGeneratorQualificationTournamentKaggleLocked12V1-v1.1.0.ps1"

$ExpectedStarterHash = `
    "1F4373CB77C52C96781323DCA4F812850884B47FA7220132F1BDE1B2344DE5FE"

if (-not (Test-Path -LiteralPath $Starter -PathType Leaf)) {
    throw "Starter missing after extraction: $Starter"
}

$ActualStarterHash = (
    Get-FileHash `
        -LiteralPath $Starter `
        -Algorithm SHA256
).Hash.ToUpperInvariant()

Write-Host "starter_sha256=$ActualStarterHash"

if ($ActualStarterHash -ne $ExpectedStarterHash) {
    throw "Starter SHA-256 mismatch. Expected=$ExpectedStarterHash Actual=$ActualStarterHash"
}

Write-Host "starter_hash_verified=true"

$Tokens = $null
$ParseErrors = $null

[System.Management.Automation.Language.Parser]::ParseFile(
    $Starter,
    [ref]$Tokens,
    [ref]$ParseErrors
) | Out-Null

if (@($ParseErrors).Count -gt 0) {
    $ParseErrors | Format-List Message, Extent
    throw "Windows PowerShell parser check failed. Tournament was NOT executed."
}

Write-Host "windows_powershell_5_1_parser_gate_passed=true"

Unblock-File `
    -LiteralPath $Starter `
    -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue

Write-Host ""
Write-Host "===== STARTING LOCKED12 TOURNAMENT =====" -ForegroundColor Cyan

& powershell.exe `
    -NoProfile `
    -ExecutionPolicy Bypass `
    -File $Starter `
    -RepoRoot "C:\A.L.I.C.E-main" `
    -VaultRoot "C:\ALICE_Vault" `
    -ReconnectMinutes 45

$TournamentExit = $LASTEXITCODE

                                        🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                  156 / 188
  ✓ Worked for 2m 2s


I found the exact failure. Nothing scientific failed, and no Kaggle job or private MC8 material was launched. The
controller stopped locally before the dispatcher entered its Kaggle-creation logic.
The bug was in my controller argument forwarding. It produced:

  --argument --mode --argument bootstrap


Python argparse sees --mode as a new option instead of the value belonging to --argument , which caused:

  argument --argument: expected one argument


The correct transport is:

  --argument=--mode
  --argument=bootstrap


The same bug would have affected later --plan , --expected-runtime-sha256 , generator, and judge arguments, so fixing only
bootstrap would have been insufficient. I patched the forwarding mechanism itself.
Also, the later PowerShell elseif / else errors are unrelated to the tournament. In an interactive PowerShell prompt, once you
submit the closing } of the if block, that statement is finished; a separately submitted elseif can no longer attach to it. I'll
avoid that pattern below.

v1.1.1 hotfix
The 12-model roster, judges, MC8 firewall, round sizes, model profiles, Ollama runtime lock, qualification core, and
authority rules are unchanged. This is only an execution-transport hotfix.
I verified that these important payloads are byte-identical to v1.1.0:

Yep - this one is fully explained, and it is not an authentication problem and not a tournament-science problem.
Your Kaggle doctor passed, your account authenticated correctly, and v1.1.1 never reached either:

 kaggle datasets create


or

 kaggle kernels push


So no remote Dataset was created, no Kernel ran, no model ran, and private MC8 never opened.
The failure was this sequence:




                                              🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                  163 / 188
 new deterministic Dataset ID
         ↓
 v1.1.1 asks Kaggle:
 datasets status <ID>
         ↓
 Dataset does not exist yet
         ↓
 Kaggle returns HTTP 403
         ↓
 v1.1.1 only recognizes:
 404 / not found / does not exist
         ↓
 misclassifies 403 as transport trouble
         ↓
 retries forever


Kaggle maintainers explicitly note that a 403 can sometimes deliberately mean “not found” rather than a permissions failure,
to prevent namespace probing. (GitHub)
And the official CLI provides datasets list -m specifically for enumerating datasets belonging to the authenticated account,
which gives us a much safer existence check. (GitHub)
So I've replaced the bad existence-probe design instead of merely adding "403" to a string list.

reappear later.

This run is actually a major step forward: the Kaggle backend itself worked correctly end-to-end. The failure is a narrow
wrapper-semantics bug, not a Kaggle, GPU, model, or tournament-design failure.
Your log proves that v1.1.2 successfully created the private Dataset, verified its files, pushed the private Kernel, saw it transition
through RUNNING to COMPLETE , downloaded the runtime archive/receipts, and then successfully deleted both the Kernel and
Dataset. Pasted text.txt The only contradictory line comes afterward:

  REMOTE WORKLOAD FAILED: SystemExit: 0

Pasted text.txt

That exact SystemExit: 0 is the bug.
The tournament stage worker ended in the conventional Python form:

  if __name__ == "__main__":
      raise SystemExit(main())


But alice-kaggle v0.2 executes scripts through runpy while catching BaseException so it can serialize remote failures.
SystemExit inherits from BaseException . Therefore even successful SystemExit(0) was caught and serialized as a failure.

The important distinction is:

  Kaggle platform           COMPLETE
  bootstrap computation     COMPLETE
  outputs downloaded        COMPLETE
  remote cleanup            COMPLETE

  wrapper interpretation WRONG
                        ↓
                 SystemExit(0)
                 marked failure

Real exceptions still propagate and fail the job. A successful main() simply returns normally, so the existing Kaggle wrapper
records ok=true .

I also made v1.1.3 salvage the successful v1.1.2 bootstrap instead of wasting another Kaggle run. It will accept that
previous bootstrap only if it independently proves all of these:

  platform_state == complete
  output_verified == true
  cleanup_complete == true
  wrapper error == SystemExit(0)
  stage_complete == true
  mode == bootstrap
  GPU count >= 2
  Ollama version == locked version
  runtime archive SHA == locked SHA
  receipt/archive hashes agree
  stage-complete/archive hashes agree
  runtime binary hashes agree


If even one condition differs, it fails closed.

output/state to timestamped preservation artifacts. It does not delete history.

The new version fixes this at the infrastructure level. Every per-job Dataset and Kernel title now goes through a deterministic 6-
50 character constructor. It preserves the full request key when possible plus the first 12 hex characters of the request hash.
For the exact request that failed, the old title was 56 characters; the new one is 49:

  OLD - invalid
  ALICE Tournament public-qualification-p1-b1 A1B2C3D4E5F6
  length=56

  NEW - valid
  ALICE T D public qualification p1 b1 A1B2C3D4E5F6
  length=49


I also fixed the retry behavior: known deterministic metadata errors such as "dataset title must be between 6 and 50
characters" now fail immediately instead of looping for 45 minutes.

Most importantly, v1.1.4 has safe predecessor-state reconciliation. The v1.1.3 pass01-batch01 dispatch state has a different
execution hash because the dispatcher itself changed. v1.1.4 will not blindly overwrite it. It first checks the old deterministic
Dataset and Kernel refs through Kaggle. Only if both are proven absent, no Kernel was ever pushed, and no output was
accepted does it rename that old state to a timestamped preservation artifact and continue. If a remote resource unexpectedly
exists, it fails closed.
The verified bootstrap and the existing shared runtime Dataset are reused. We are not spending another GPU job on bootstrap.
The scientific tournament remains frozen and byte-identical:

The new failure is a different Kaggle identity rule. Kaggle explicitly warned:

  Your kernel title does not resolve to the specified id.


Then the controller kept checking the declared slug, found nothing, retried the push, and got HTTP 409 Conflict . Pasted
text.txt

Kaggle's own kernel-metadata documentation confirms why: a Kernel's slug is linked to its title-the slug is derived from the title by
lowercasing it and replacing spaces with dashes. (GitHub)
So v1.1.4 had:

  declared Kernel ID slug
  alice-tourn-public-qualification-p1-b1-b104d2




                                            🚀 Powered by ChatGPT Exporter                                                                     180 / 188
but a title equivalent to:

  ALICE T K public qualification p1 b1 B104D2355ED2


which Kaggle resolves to:

  alice-t-k-public-qualification-p1-b1-b104d2355ed2


Those are different. The first push likely created the private Kernel under the title-derived slug, while our controller kept
polling the other slug. The subsequent 409 is exactly consistent with that.
Your Ctrl+C was therefore the right move. Pasted text.txt

Fixed: LOCKED12 v1.1.5
I fixed this at the identity layer, not with another one-off name change.
Kernel titles now derive directly from the deterministic Kernel ID:

  ID slug
  alice-tourn-public-qualification-p1-b1-XXXXXX

  title
  alice tourn public qualification p1 b1 XXXXXX

  Kaggle-derived slug
  alice-tourn-public-qualification-p1-b1-XXXXXX

                              EXACT MATCH ✓


Before every Kernel push, v1.1.5 locally asserts:

  slug(kernel_title) == declared_kernel_id_slug


If that invariant fails, it stops before touching Kaggle.
I also removed the dangerous retry behavior for deterministic Kernel errors. Title/ID mismatch, invalid metadata, and 409
Conflict are now fatal rather than being retried as if they were Wi-Fi failures.

More importantly, I accounted for the remote state v1.1.4 may have left behind. v1.1.5 will automatically:

1. Read the preserved v1.1.4 dispatch state.
2. Derive the possible title-created Kernel:
    mkrayanyan/alice-t-k-public-qualification-p1-b1-b104d2355ed2 .

3. Prove the intended old Kernel ID is absent.
4. Find and delete the title-derived Kernel if it exists, including if it is still running.
5. Wait until Kaggle actually reports that Kernel absent.
6. Delete and prove absent the superseded per-job Dataset:
    mkrayanyan/alice-tourn-public-qualification-p1-b1-b104d2-data .

7. Preserve the old state under a timestamped historical filename.
8. Leave the shared runtime Dataset completely untouched.
9. Rerun that public-only qualification batch under the corrected v1.1.5 identity.

So do not manually delete anything on Kaggle first.

  qual_core_v1.py
